\bm{V}_{i}
,
\bm{W}_{i}
,
Z_{0i}
are mutually independent. With

a_{i}=\sqrt{p}Z_{0i}\sqrt{B_{i}},\qquad b_{i}=\sqrt{p}Z_{0i}\sqrt{1-B_{i}},\qquad\mathcal{G}_{n,J}=\sigma\{(Z_{0i},B_{i}):1\leq i\leq n\},

\bm{S}_{n,J}^{0}=n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\bm{V}_{i},\qquad Q_{n,J}=\frac{2}{n\sqrt{2p}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq n}b_{i}b_{k}\bm{W}_{i}^{\top}\bm{W}_{k},

H_{n}^{0}/\sqrt{2p}=Q_{n,J}+\Delta_{n,J},\qquad\Delta_{n,J}=\frac{1}{n\sqrt{2p}}\sum_{1\leq i<k\leq n}2a_{i}a_{k}\bm{V}_{i}^{\top}\bm{V}_{k}.

Conditional on
\mathcal{G}_{n,J}
,
\bm{S}_{n,J}^{0}
and
Q_{n,J}
are independent. For every fixed
d_{0},d_{1}
,

\mathbb{E}\Delta_{n,J}^{2}\leq C_{d}p^{-1},\qquad\sup_{t}\left|\mathbb{P}(Q_{n,J}\leq t)-\Phi(t)\right|\leq C_{d}\left(n^{-1/2}+p^{-1/2}\right),

\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}\{E_{J_{0},J_{1}}(y)\mid\mathcal{G}_{n,J}\}}{q_{0n}(y)^{d_{0}}q_{1n}(y)^{d_{1}}}-1\right|\leq C_{d}\left\{\frac{(\log p)^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{(\log p)^{2}}{p}+\frac{[b_{n,p}^{(0)}]^{2}\log p\log n}{n}+e^{-2\mu_{n}u_{p}(y)}\right\}

with probability tending to one. Hence, with

\varepsilon_{n,d}=C_{d}\left\{\frac{(\log p)^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{(\log p)^{2}}{p}+\frac{[b_{n,p}^{(0)}]^{2}\log p\log n}{n}+e^{-2\mu_{n}u_{p}(y)}+p^{-1/2}+n^{-1/2}\right\},

\varepsilon_{n,d}\to 0,\qquad\sup_{J_{0},J_{1}}\left|\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(H_{n}^{0}/\sqrt{2p}\leq x,E_{J_{0},J_{1}}(y)\right)}{\Phi(x)q_{0n}(y)^{d_{0}}q_{1n}(y)^{d_{1}}}-1\right|\leq\varepsilon_{n,d}+o(1).

Equivalently,

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{H_{n}^{0}}{\sqrt{2p}}\leq x,E_{J_{0},J_{1}}(y)\right)=\Phi(x)q_{0n}(y)^{d_{0}}q_{1n}(y)^{d_{1}}\{1+r_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}\},

\sup_{J_{0},J_{1}}|r_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}|\leq\varepsilon_{n,d}+o(1)\to 0.

The active lower tail is negligible:

\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y)+\mu_{n})\leq\exp\{-2\mu_{n}u_{p}(y)\}\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n}),\qquad s_{n}\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y)+\mu_{n})\to 0.

Moreover,

u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n}=u_{s_{n}}(\eta)+u_{p}(y)-u_{p}(0),

\{u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n}\}^{2}=u_{s_{n}}(\eta)^{2}+2u_{s_{n}}(\eta)\{u_{p}(y)-u_{p}(0)\}+\{u_{p}(y)-u_{p}(0)\}^{2},

2u_{s_{n}}(\eta)\{u_{p}(y)-u_{p}(0)\}=2u_{s_{n}}(\eta)\frac{y}{u_{p}(y)+u_{p}(0)}\to\frac{y}{\sqrt{2}},\qquad\{u_{p}(y)-u_{p}(0)\}^{2}\to 0.

For every
t\to\infty
,

\bar{\Phi}(t)=\frac{1}{t\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-t^{2}/2)\{1+O(t^{-2})\}.

Thus

s_{n}\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n})=\frac{s_{n}}{\{u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n}\}\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\{u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n}\}^{2}\right]\{1+O((\log p)^{-1})\},

s_{n}\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y)-\mu_{n})\to\lambda_{1}(y;\eta)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\exp\left(-\frac{\eta}{2}-\frac{y}{2\sqrt{2}}\right),

(p-s_{n})2\bar{\Phi}(u_{p}(y))\to\lambda_{0}(y)=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-y/2}.

Set

A_{n}(x)=\left\{\frac{H_{n}^{0}}{\sqrt{2p}}\leq x\right\},\qquad L_{n}(y)=\left\{\max_{1\leq j\leq p}|S_{n,j}^{0}+a_{nj}|\leq u_{p}(y)\right\}.

For fixed
m\geq 1
, Bonferroni’s inequalities give

\sum_{d=0}^{2m+1}(-1)^{d}\sum_{d_{0}+d_{1}=d}C_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}(x,y)\leq\mathbb{P}\{A_{n}(x)\cap L_{n}(y)\}\leq\sum_{d=0}^{2m}(-1)^{d}\sum_{d_{0}+d_{1}=d}C_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}(x,y),

where

C_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}(x,y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J_{0}\subset A_{n}^{c},|J_{0}|=d_{0}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J_{1}\subset A_{n},|J_{1}|=d_{1}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}\{A_{n}(x)\cap E_{J_{0},J_{1}}(y)\}.

For fixed
d_{0},d_{1}
,

C_{n,d_{0},d_{1}}(x,y)\to\Phi(x)\frac{\lambda_{0}(y)^{d_{0}}}{d_{0}!}\frac{\lambda_{1}(y;\eta)^{d_{1}}}{d_{1}!}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\{A_{n}(x)\cap L_{n}(y)\}\to\Phi(x)\sum_{d_{0}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{d_{1}=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{d_{0}+d_{1}}\frac{\lambda_{0}(y)^{d_{0}}}{d_{0}!}\frac{\lambda_{1}(y;\eta)^{d_{1}}}{d_{1}!},

\mathbb{P}\{A_{n}(x)\cap L_{n}(y)\}\to\Phi(x)\exp\{-\lambda_{0}(y)-\lambda_{1}(y;\eta)\}.

Thus

\left(\frac{H_{n}^{0}}{\sqrt{2p}},\|\bm{S}_{n}^{0}+\bm{a}_{n}\|_{\infty}^{2}-2\log p+\log\log p\right)\xrightarrow{d}(Z,G_{\eta}),\qquad Z\mathrel{\perp\mspace{-10mu}\perp}G_{\eta}.

The reductions above imply

\left(\frac{T_{\rm sum}-p}{\sqrt{2p}},T_{\max}\right)\xrightarrow{d}(Z+\theta_{S},G_{\eta}),\qquad Z\mathrel{\perp\mspace{-10mu}\perp}G_{\eta}.

By Proposition
3.2
,

|\hat{T}_{\rm sum}-T_{\rm sum}|\leq 2n\|\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{2}\|\hat{\bm{g}}_{n}^{(1)}-\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{2}+n\|\hat{\bm{g}}_{n}^{(1)}-\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{2}^{2},

\frac{|\hat{T}_{\rm sum}-T_{\rm sum}|}{\sqrt{p}}=O_{p}\left(\frac{n\{(\|\bm{\gamma}\|_{2}+\sqrt{p/n})\mathfrak{R}_{2,n}^{(1)}+[\mathfrak{R}_{2,n}^{(1)}]^{2}\}}{\sqrt{p}}\right)=o_{p}(1),

|\hat{T}_{\max}-T_{\max}|\leq 2n\|\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\bm{g}}_{n}^{(1)}-\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{\infty}+n\|\hat{\bm{g}}_{n}^{(1)}-\bm{g}_{n}^{(1),\rm or}\|_{\infty}^{2},

|\hat{T}_{\max}-T_{\max}|=O_{p}\left(n\{(\|\bm{\gamma}\|_{\infty}+\sqrt{\log p/n})\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,n}^{(1)}+[\mathfrak{R}_{\infty,n}^{(1)}]^{2}\}\right)=o_{p}(1).

Hence

\left(\frac{\hat{T}_{\rm sum}-p}{\sqrt{2p}},\hat{T}_{\max}\right)\xrightarrow{d}(Z+\theta_{S},G_{\eta}),\qquad Z\mathrel{\perp\mspace{-10mu}\perp}G_{\eta}.

Let
c_{\alpha}=F_{G}^{-1}(1-\alpha)
and
z_{1-\alpha}=\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha)
. Since

\mathbb{P}(Z+\theta_{S}>z_{1-\alpha})=1-\Phi(z_{1-\alpha}-\theta_{S}),

\mathbb{P}(G_{\eta}>c_{\alpha})=1-F_{\eta}(c_{\alpha}),\qquad F_{G}(c_{\alpha})=\exp\{-\lambda_{0}(c_{\alpha})\}=1-\alpha,

F_{\eta}(c_{\alpha})=F_{G}(c_{\alpha})\exp\{-\lambda_{1}(c_{\alpha};\eta)\}<1-\alpha,

we obtain

\alpha<1-\Phi(z_{1-\alpha}-\theta_{S})<1,\qquad\alpha<1-F_{\eta}(c_{\alpha})<1.

∎

Appendix B
Verification of Assumption
3.1
for Common Elliptical Radial Laws

This appendix verifies Assumption
3.1
(ii) for the radial laws used in the simulations and for several standard elliptical models. Assumption
3.1
(i) is part of the elliptical construction in all examples below. Write

H_{n,p}=(\log p)^{5}+\log p\log n.

The constants denoted by
C,c,C_{b}
are positive and do not depend on
n
or
p
;
C_{b}
is taken sufficiently large.

Let
G_{a}\sim\Gamma(a,1)
and

W_{a}=\frac{\log G_{a}-\psi(a)}{\sqrt{\psi_{1}(a)}},

where
\psi
and
\psi_{1}
are the digamma and trigamma functions. Since

\mathbb{E}\log G_{a}=\psi(a),\qquad\mathrm{Var}(\log G_{a})=\psi_{1}(a),\qquad\psi_{1}(a)=a^{-1}+O(a^{-2}),

W_{a}
has mean zero and variance one. For
0\leq\lambda\leq a/2
,

\mathbb{E}\exp\{\lambda(\log G_{a}-\psi(a))\}=\exp\{\log\Gamma(a+\lambda)-\log\Gamma(a)-\lambda\psi(a)\},

\log\Gamma(a+\lambda)-\log\Gamma(a)-\lambda\psi(a)=\int_{0}^{\lambda}(\lambda-t)\psi_{1}(a+t)\,dt\leq C\lambda^{2}/a,

and similarly, for
0\leq\lambda\leq a/2
,

\mathbb{E}\exp\{-\lambda(\log G_{a}-\psi(a))\}=\exp\{\log\Gamma(a-\lambda)-\log\Gamma(a)+\lambda\psi(a)\}\leq\exp(C\lambda^{2}/a).

Therefore, for all
x\geq 0
,

\Pr\{|\log G_{a}-\psi(a)|>x\}\leq C\exp\{-c\min(ax^{2},ax)\}.

With
x=t\sqrt{\psi_{1}(a)}
,

\Pr(|W_{a}|>t)\leq C\exp\{-c\min(t^{2},\sqrt{a}\,t)\},\qquad t\geq 0.

Consequently, for every fixed
q>0
,

\mathbb{E}|W_{a}|^{q}=q\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}\Pr(|W_{a}|>t)\,dt\leq C_{q}.

If
a\geq cp
, then the deterministic envelope

b^{(G)}_{n,p}=C_{b}\left\{\sqrt{\log(en)}+\frac{\log(en)}{\sqrt{p}}\right\}

satisfies

\Pr\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq n}|W_{a,i}|>b^{(G)}_{n,p}\right)\leq Cn\exp\left[-c\min\{(b^{(G)}_{n,p})^{2},\sqrt{p}\,b^{(G)}_{n,p}\}\right]\to 0,

provided
C_{b}
is large enough. Moreover,

\frac{(b^{(G)}_{n,p})^{2}H_{n,p}}{n}\leq\frac{C\{\log(en)+p^{-1}(\log(en))^{2}\}H_{n,p}}{n}.

Thus the gamma-log radial laws below satisfy Assumption
3.1
(ii) whenever

\frac{\{\log(en)+p^{-1}(\log(en))^{2}\}H_{n,p}}{n}\to 0.

For the Gaussian elliptical model,

\bm{Y}\sim N(0,\mathbf{I}_{p}),\qquad R^{2}\sim\chi_{p}^{2}=2G_{p/2},\qquad L=\log R=2^{-1}\log 2+2^{-1}\log G_{p/2}.

Hence

m_{L}=2^{-1}\{\log 2+\psi(p/2)\},\qquad\sigma_{L}^{2}=4^{-1}\psi_{1}(p/2)\asymp p^{-1},\qquad Z=W_{p/2}.

Therefore one may take

b_{n,p}=C_{b}\left\{\sqrt{\log(en)}+\frac{\log(en)}{\sqrt{p}}\right\}.

For the Kotz-type power-exponential radial law used in the simulations,

R_{0}=(2G_{p/(2\beta)})^{1/(2\beta)},\qquad R=c_{p,\beta}R_{0},\qquad\mathbb{E}R^{2}=p,

where
\beta>0
is fixed and
c_{p,\beta}
is deterministic. Since deterministic scaling only adds a constant to
L=\log R
,

L=\log c_{p,\beta}+\frac{1}{2\beta}\log 2+\frac{1}{2\beta}\log G_{p/(2\beta)},

\sigma_{L}^{2}=\frac{1}{4\beta^{2}}\psi_{1}\{p/(2\beta)\}\asymp p^{-1},\qquad Z=W_{p/(2\beta)}.

Thus the same envelope is admissible:

b_{n,p}=C_{b}\left\{\sqrt{\log(en)}+\frac{\log(en)}{\sqrt{p}}\right\}.

For the bounded radial law,

R^{2}=2pB,\qquad B\sim\mathrm{Beta}(p/2,p/2).

Let
G_{1},G_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\Gamma(p/2,1)
and
B=G_{1}/(G_{1}+G_{2})
. Then

L=2^{-1}\log(2p)+2^{-1}\log B,\qquad m_{L}=2^{-1}\log(2p)+2^{-1}\{\psi(p/2)-\psi(p)\},

\sigma_{L}^{2}=4^{-1}\{\psi_{1}(p/2)-\psi_{1}(p)\}\asymp p^{-1}.

Moreover,

\log B-\mathbb{E}\log B=\{\log G_{1}-\psi(p/2)\}-\{\log(G_{1}+G_{2})-\psi(p)\},

where
G_{1}+G_{2}\sim\Gamma(p,1)
. Hence

\Pr(|Z|>t)\leq\Pr(|W_{p/2}|>ct)+\Pr(|W_{p}|>ct)\leq C\exp\{-c\min(t^{2},\sqrt{p}\,t)\},

and one may again take

b_{n,p}=C_{b}\left\{\sqrt{\log(en)}+\frac{\log(en)}{\sqrt{p}}\right\}.

For the multivariate
t_{\nu}
elliptical model with fixed
\nu>0
,

\bm{Y}=\frac{\bm{Z}}{\sqrt{G_{\nu}/\nu}},\qquad\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\mathbf{I}_{p}),\qquad G_{\nu}\sim\chi_{\nu}^{2},

with
\bm{Z}
independent of
G_{\nu}
. Then

L=2^{-1}\log\chi_{p}^{2}-2^{-1}\log(G_{\nu}/\nu),

\sigma_{L}^{2}=4^{-1}\psi_{1}(p/2)+4^{-1}\psi_{1}(\nu/2)\asymp 1.

The first term has the gamma-log bound above. The second term is a fixed-dimensional log-gamma variable. Hence, for constants depending only on
\nu
,

\Pr(|Z|>t)\leq C_{\nu}e^{-c_{\nu}t}+C\exp\{-c\min(t^{2},\sqrt{p}\,t)\},\qquad t\geq 0.

Therefore

\sup_{p}\mathbb{E}|Z|^{8+\eta_{Z}}<\infty

for every fixed
\eta_{Z}>0
, and

b_{n,p}=C_{b}\log(en)

is admissible. The truncation-rate requirement becomes

\frac{(\log(en))^{2}H_{n,p}}{n}\to 0.

For a bounded non-degenerate Gaussian scale mixture,

\bm{Y}=S\bm{Z},\qquad\bm{Z}\sim N(0,\mathbf{I}_{p}),\qquad 0<s_{-}\leq S\leq s_{+}<\infty,\qquad\mathrm{Var}(\log S)>0,

with
S
independent of
\bm{Z}
,

L=\log S+2^{-1}\log\chi_{p}^{2},

\sigma_{L}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(\log S)+4^{-1}\psi_{1}(p/2)\asymp 1.

Since
\log S
is bounded and the Gaussian log-radius part satisfies the gamma-log bound,

\sup_{p}\mathbb{E}|Z|^{8+\eta_{Z}}<\infty,\qquad b_{n,p}=C_{b}\log(en)

is admissible, again under

\frac{(\log(en))^{2}H_{n,p}}{n}\to 0.

The two-point mixture used in the simulations,
\Pr(S=1)=0.9
and
\Pr(S=3)=0.1
, is a special case.

The following table summarizes the resulting choices of
b_{n,p}
.

2.6

1.2

The complete size table shows that the proposed max component is particularly stable under non-concentrated radial laws, while the sum component can be mildly liberal for the
p=400
mixture-normal and
t_{10}
settings when the purely analytic calibration is used. This is a finite-sample effect of estimating many weak coordinate correlations under strong radial variability. The Cauchy combination moderates this behavior and remains closer to the nominal level in most heavy-tailed cases. These entries motivate the optional bootstrap mean–variance correction in Section
2.4
, but they do not change the main qualitative conclusion: the radial–directional tests maintain usable calibration across the five null radial laws and the three shape structures. The table also separates two sources of difficulty. Changing
\mathbf{\Sigma}
affects the quality of the plug-in standardization, whereas changing the radial law affects the variability of the log-radius. The fact that the empirical sizes remain broadly comparable across both dimensions supports the modular structure of the method: HR standardization handles the unknown affine geometry, and the correlation statistic handles the radial–directional diagnostic.

C.1
Complete empirical power tables

Tables in this subsection report the complete power results. The method labels S, M, C and W denote HR-Sum, HR-Max, HR-Cauchy and WL, respectively. The rows exclude
\delta_{n}=0
, which corresponds to the null model and is summarized in Table
8
. The five radial laws produce different values of
\sigma_{0,n}^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(\log R_{0i})
, so the same numerical value of
\delta_{n}
does not imply the same signal-to-noise ratio. This is why the Gaussian, Kotz-type and bounded-radial baselines are easier than the mixture-normal and
t_{10}
baselines at the same
p
and
\delta_{n}
.

46

The complete power tables confirm the main message of Table
3
. In sparse alternatives, M is usually the leading component, especially when
A=A_{\rm sp}
. In moderately dense and fully dense alternatives, S dominates M because the coordinatewise correlations are individually weaker but accumulate across many active coordinates. The Cauchy statistic is rarely far from the stronger of S and M and is therefore the most robust default choice. Across radial laws, the proposed tests consistently exploit the targeted radial–directional signal more effectively than W, whose projection-based construction is not tailored to the dependence pattern in (
18
). The detailed rows further show that the shape structure changes the absolute power only moderately after HR standardization, whereas the active-set size and radial variability have the dominant effects. This supports the interpretation of S and M as complementary diagnostics rather than competing versions of the same statistic.

Appendix D
Additional Power Figures

Figures in this section give the single-radial-law power summaries. Each figure averages over the three shape structures and displays the same three alternatives and three dimensions used in the power study. The horizontal axis is the signal strength
\delta_{n}
in (
18
). These plots are intended to complement the tables by showing how quickly each component reacts as radial–directional dependence increases.

Figure 3:
Additional empirical power curves for the Gaussian baseline. The concentrated log-radius makes the radial–directional signal strong, so S and C rise quickly for dense alternatives and M rises quickly for sparse alternatives.

Figure 4:
Additional empirical power curves for the
t_{10}
baseline. Heavy radial variability makes this setting harder, but the same sparse-versus-dense pattern is visible as
\delta_{n}
increases.

Figure 5:
Additional empirical power curves for the Kotz-type baseline with
\beta=2
. This concentrated radial law behaves similarly to the Gaussian baseline after log-radius standardization.

Figure 6:
Additional empirical power curves for the bounded-radial baseline. The results show that the tests do not rely on Gaussian radial tails and remain powerful when the radius has bounded support.

Figure 7:
Additional empirical power curves for the mixture-normal baseline. The non-concentrated scale mixture reduces the signal-to-noise ratio, but C continues to adapt between the dense S component and the sparse M component.

Appendix E
Additional Application Results

Table
14
gives selected supplementary application blocks used to support the discussion in Section
5
. The supplementary screening uses the same preprocessing principle as the main real-data analysis: variables are extracted within a fixed block, non-finite or constant columns are removed if necessary, and the remaining columns are standardized before the tests are computed. For Arcene, the blocks are variance-screened feature sets from the pooled training and validation samples of the UCI mass-spectrometry data
(
12
;
11
)
. For cookie, the blocks are high-wavelength tail windows of the biscuit-dough NIR spectra
(

;
29
)
. For glass, the blocks are local EPXMA spectral channels from the archaeological glass data used in robust sparse PCA studies
(
21
;
16
)
. The table emphasizes blocks where the radial–directional tests provide additional local or sparse-versus-dense interpretation.
